src/rlang/vec.h:144:static inline r_obj* r_list_get(r_obj* x, r_ssize i) {

  r_ssize v_point[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  // Since coalescing can multiply two axes' dimensions together, we use an
  // `r_ssize` here even though an individual dimension can't be above an `int`.
  r_ssize v_dimensions[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  int dimensionality;

  r_ssize start;
  r_ssize end;
  r_ssize v_loc[RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];

  // Strides for all `n` arrays, laid out axis-major in a flat array as
  // [dimensionality][n], allowing contiguous access when doing "next" calls.
  r_ssize v_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY * RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];
  r_ssize n;
};

static inline struct rray_run_iterator rray_run_iterator(
  const int* v_dimensions,
  int dimensionality,
  const r_ssize* const* v_v_strides,
  r_ssize n
) {
  check_dimensionality(dimensionality);

  if (n < 1 || n > RRAY_MAX_INPUTS) {
    r_stop_internal(
      "`n` (%" R_PRI_SSIZE ") must be between 1 and %d.",
      n,
      RRAY_MAX_INPUTS
    );
  }

  struct rray_run_iterator it;

  it.size = rray_size_from_dimensions_checked(
    v_dimensions,
    dimensionality,
    r_lazy_null
  );

  for (int axis = 0; axis < dimensionality; ++axis) {
    it.v_dimensions[axis] = (r_ssize) v_dimensions[axis];
  }

  // Reorder strides to be contiguous during next() calls
  for (int axis = 0; axis < dimensionality; ++axis) {
    r_ssize* v_strides = it.v_strides + axis * n;
    for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
      v_strides[i] = v_v_strides[i][axis];
    }
  }

  it.dimensionality = rray__run_iterator_axes_coalesce(
    it.v_dimensions,
    it.v_strides,
    n,
    dimensionality
  );

  it.start = 0;
  it.end = it.v_dimensions[0];

  it.n = n;

  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
    it.v_loc[i] = 0;
  }

  for (int axis = 0; axis < it.dimensionality; ++axis) {
    it.v_point[axis] = 0;
  }

  return it;
}

static inline struct rray_run_iterator rray_run_iterator1(
  const int* v_dimensions,
  int dimensionality,
  const r_ssize* v_strides
) {
  const r_ssize* v_v_strides[] = {v_strides};
  return rray_run_iterator(v_dimensions, dimensionality, v_v_strides, 1);
}

  const int* v_dimensions,
  int dimensionality,
  const r_ssize* v_strides1,
  const r_ssize* v_strides2
) {

+  it.n = n;
+
+  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
+    it.v_loc[i] = 0;
+  }
+
+  for (int axis = 0; axis < it.dimensionality; ++axis) {
+    it.v_point[axis] = 0;
+  }
+
+  return it;
+}

+static inline void rray_run_iterator_reset(struct rray_run_iterator* it) {
+  it->start = 0;
+  it->end = it->v_dimensions[0];
+
+  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < it->n; ++i) {
+    it->v_loc[i] = 0;
+  }
+
+  for (int axis = 0; axis < it->dimensionality; ++axis) {
+    it->v_point[axis] = 0;
+  }

77	  // Initialized on the first iteration. Changes any time the output dimension
78	  // along `axis` changes, but in the uniform case all arrays share the same
79	  // dimensions object!
80	  r_obj* out_elt_dimensions = r_null;
81	  r_keep_loc out_elt_dimensions_loc;
82	  KEEP_HERE(out_elt_dimensions, &out_elt_dimensions_loc);
83	
84	  int* v_out_elt_dimensions = NULL;
85	
86	  int previous_dimension = -1;
87	
88	  // The location along the axis. For example, with a 2x10 array split along
89	  // columns this runs from 0-9. The `x_axis_stride` maps it to `x_start`, a
90	  // flat location into `x` itself.
91	  int x_axis_start = 0;
92	
93	  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < out_size; ++i) {
94	    const int dimension = uniform ? v_dimensions[0] : v_dimensions[i];
95	
96	    if (dimension != previous_dimension) {
97	      previous_dimension = dimension;
98	
99	      out_elt_dimensions = r_alloc_integer(dimensionality);
100	      KEEP_AT(out_elt_dimensions, out_elt_dimensions_loc);
101	      v_out_elt_dimensions = r_int_begin(out_elt_dimensions);
102	      r_memcpy(
103	        v_out_elt_dimensions,
104	        v_x_dimensions,
105	        sizeof(int) * dimensionality
106	      );
107	      v_out_elt_dimensions[axis - 1] = dimension;
108	    }
109	
110	    const r_ssize x_start = x_axis_start * x_axis_stride;
111	
112	    r_obj* out_elt = rray_split_elt(
113	      x,
114	      x_start,
115	      v_out_elt_dimensions,
116	      dimensionality,
117	      v_x_strides
118	    );
119	    r_list_poke(out, i, out_elt);
120	    r_attrib_poke_dim(out_elt, out_elt_dimensions);
121	
122	    if (v_x_axis_names != NULL) {
123	      r_obj* out_elt_names = KEEP(rray_split_elt_names(
124	        v_x_names,
125	        dimensionality,
126	        axis,
127	        v_x_axis_names,
128	        x_axis_start,
129	        dimension
130	      ));
131	      r_attrib_poke_dim_names(out_elt, out_elt_names);
132	      FREE(1);
133	    } else if (x_names != r_null) {
134	      r_attrib_poke_dim_names(out_elt, x_names);
135	    }
136	
137	    x_axis_start += dimension;
138	  }
139	
140	  FREE(5);
141	  return out;

202	static r_obj* rray_split_elt(
203	  r_obj* x,
204	  r_ssize x_start,
205	  const int* v_out_elt_dimensions,
206	  int dimensionality,
207	  const r_ssize* v_x_strides
208	) {
209	  switch (r_typeof(x)) {
210	  case R_TYPE_logical:
211	    return rray_split_elt_lgl(
212	      x,
213	      x_start,
214	      v_out_elt_dimensions,
215	      dimensionality,
216	      v_x_strides
217	    );
218	  case R_TYPE_integer:
219	    return rray_split_elt_int(
220	      x,
221	      x_start,
222	      v_out_elt_dimensions,
223	      dimensionality,
224	      v_x_strides
225	    );
226	  case R_TYPE_double:
227	    return rray_split_elt_dbl(
228	      x,
229	      x_start,
230	      v_out_elt_dimensions,
231	      dimensionality,
232	      v_x_strides
233	    );
234	  case R_TYPE_complex:
235	    return rray_split_elt_cpl(
236	      x,
237	      x_start,
238	      v_out_elt_dimensions,
239	      dimensionality,
240	      v_x_strides
241	    );
242	  case R_TYPE_raw:
243	    return rray_split_elt_raw(
244	      x,
245	      x_start,
246	      v_out_elt_dimensions,
247	      dimensionality,
248	      v_x_strides
249	    );
250	  case R_TYPE_character:
251	    return rray_split_elt_chr(
252	      x,
253	      x_start,
254	      v_out_elt_dimensions,
255	      dimensionality,
256	      v_x_strides
257	    );
258	  case R_TYPE_list:
259	    return rray_split_elt_list(
260	      x,
261	      x_start,
262	      v_out_elt_dimensions,
263	      dimensionality,
264	      v_x_strides
265	    );
266	  default:
267	    r_stop_unreachable();
268	  }
269	}

271	#define RRAY_SPLIT_ELT_LOOP(POKE)                                              \
272	  for (; !rray_run_iterator_done(&it); rray_run_iterator_next1(&it)) {         \
273	    const r_ssize start = rray_run_iterator_start(&it);                        \
274	    const r_ssize end = rray_run_iterator_end(&it);                            \
275	                                                                               \
276	    r_ssize x_loc = x_start + rray_run_iterator_loc(&it, 0);                   \
277	    const r_ssize x_stride = rray_run_iterator_stride(&it, 0);                 \
278	                                                                               \
279	    for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {                                    \
280	      POKE(out, i, v_x[x_loc]);                                                \
281	      x_loc += x_stride;                                                       \
282	    }                                                                          \
283	  }
284	
285	#define RRAY_SPLIT_ELT_ATOMIC_POKE(OUT, I, VALUE) v_out[I] = (VALUE)
286	
287	#define RRAY_SPLIT_ELT_ATOMIC(RTYPE, CTYPE, CONST_DEREF, DEREF)                \
288	  struct rray_run_iterator it =                                                \
289	    rray_run_iterator1(v_out_elt_dimensions, dimensionality, v_x_strides);     \
290	                                                                               \
291	  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_alloc_vector(RTYPE, rray_run_iterator_size(&it)));       \
292	  CTYPE* v_out = DEREF(out);                                                   \
293	                                                                               \
294	  const CTYPE* v_x = CONST_DEREF(x);                                           \
295	                                                                               \
296	  RRAY_SPLIT_ELT_LOOP(RRAY_SPLIT_ELT_ATOMIC_POKE);                             \
297	                                                                               \
298	  FREE(1);                                                                     \
299	  return out;
300	
301	#define RRAY_SPLIT_ELT_BARRIER(RTYPE, CONST_DEREF, POKE)                       \
302	  struct rray_run_iterator it =                                                \
303	    rray_run_iterator1(v_out_elt_dimensions, dimensionality, v_x_strides);     \
304	                                                                               \
305	  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_alloc_vector(RTYPE, rray_run_iterator_size(&it)));       \
306	                                                                               \
307	  r_obj* const* v_x = CONST_DEREF(x);                                          \
308	                                                                               \
309	  RRAY_SPLIT_ELT_LOOP(POKE);                                                   \

Read src/split.c 
74	  );
75	  const r_ssize x_axis_stride = v_x_strides[axis - 1];
76	
77	  // Initialized on the first iteration. Changes any time the output dimension
78	  // along `axis` changes, but in the uniform case all arrays share the same
79	  // dimensions object!
80	  r_obj* out_elt_dimensions = r_null;
81	  r_keep_loc out_elt_dimensions_loc;
82	  KEEP_HERE(out_elt_dimensions, &out_elt_dimensions_loc);
83	
84	  r_ssize group_end = 0;
85	
86	  // The location along the axis. For example, with a 2x10 array split along
87	  // columns this runs from 0-9. The `x_axis_stride` maps it to `x_start`, a
88	  // flat location into `x` itself.
89	  int x_axis_start = 0;
90	
91	  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < out_size; ++i) {
92	    const int dimension = uniform ? v_dimensions[0] : v_dimensions[i];
93	
94	    if (i == group_end) {
95	      group_end = i + 1;
96	
97	      while (group_end < out_size &&
98	             (uniform || v_dimensions[group_end] == dimension)) {
99	        ++group_end;
100	      }
101	
102	      out_elt_dimensions = r_alloc_integer(dimensionality);
103	      KEEP_AT(out_elt_dimensions, out_elt_dimensions_loc);
104	      int* v_out_elt_dimensions = r_int_begin(out_elt_dimensions);
105	      r_memcpy(
106	        v_out_elt_dimensions,
107	        v_x_dimensions,
108	        sizeof(int) * dimensionality
109	      );
110	      v_out_elt_dimensions[axis - 1] = dimension;
111	
112	      const r_ssize x_start = x_axis_start * x_axis_stride;
113	      const r_ssize x_step = dimension * x_axis_stride;
114	
115	      rray_split_fill(
116	        x,
117	        out,
118	        i,
119	        group_end,
120	        x_start,
121	        x_step,
122	        v_out_elt_dimensions,
123	        dimensionality,
124	        v_x_strides
125	      );
126	    }
127	
128	    r_obj* out_elt = r_list_get(out, i);
129	    r_attrib_poke_dim(out_elt, out_elt_dimensions);
130	
131	    if (v_x_axis_names != NULL) {
132	      r_obj* out_elt_names = KEEP(rray_split_elt_names(
133	        v_x_names,
134	        dimensionality,
135	        axis,
136	        v_x_axis_names,
137	        x_axis_start,
138	        dimension
139	      ));
140	      r_attrib_poke_dim_names(out_elt, out_elt_names);
141	      FREE(1);
142	    } else if (x_names != r_null) {
143	      r_attrib_poke_dim_names(out_elt, x_names);
144	    }
145	
146	    x_axis_start += dimension;
147	  }
148	
149	  FREE(5);
150	  return out;
151	}

Write ~/.claude/projects/-Users-davis-files-r-packages-rray4/memory/project_many_small_o… 
---
name: project-many-small-outputs
description: For kernels producing many small arrays (split, unstack), build one run iterator and reset it, and set attributes in the same pass
metadata:
  type: project
---

When a function produces many small output arrays (like `rray_split()` into 1e5 columns), two things cost 5% to 22% each against the old plan-based code:

- Building a `rray_run_iterator` per output array (about 6 to 10 ns each: coalescing plus small `memcpy()` / `bzero()` calls). Fix: build it once per distinct dimension inside the typed kernel and call `rray_run_iterator_reset()` between arrays.

- Allocating every array first, then setting `dim` in a second pass. Revisiting 1e5 objects misses the cache. Fix: set `dim` in the kernel right after allocation.

**Why:** found while moving `rray_split()` to the run iterator (Oct 2026, branch `feature/split-iterator`).

**How to apply:** use the same shape for any similar conversion. Benchmark the many-tiny-arrays case against main, see [[project-benchmarking-against-main]].
File created successfully at: ~/.claude/projects/-Users-davis-files-r-packages-rray4/memory/project_many_small_outputs.md
Edit ~/.claude/projects/-Users-davis-files-r-packages-rray4/memory/MEMORY.md 
-- [project_benchmarking_against_main.md]
+- [project_many_small_outputs.md](project_many_small_outputs.md) - Many small outputs: build the iterator once and reset it, and set `dim` in the same pass
+- [project_benchmarking_against_main.md]
The file ~/.claude/projects/-Users-davis-files-r-packages-rray4/memory/MEMORY.md has been updated successfully.

rray_split() now uses the run iterator and is as fast as main in every benchmark case. All 3497 tests pass. It's committed as aaf491c ("Move rray_split() to the run iterator") on feature/split-iterator and pushed. I didn't open a PR.

What changed

  • src/strided-iterator2.h: new rray_run_iterator_reset(), which puts an iterator back at the start of its walk. It's defined after the constructor wrappers so it needs no declaration. The decl header is included before the struct is defined, so it couldn't declare it anyway. Adding it left the machine code for add, broadcast, combine, permute-axes and sum byte-for-byte identical.

  • src/split.c: each typed kernel (rray_split_fill_dbl() and the others) now handles a range of output arrays that share the same dimensions:

    • It builds one iterator for the range.
    • For each array, it allocates it, puts it in the list, sets dim, fills it, then resets the iterator.
    • In the uniform case that's one iterator for the whole call, like main.
    • The kernels are written in the same macro style as combine and permute-axes.
    • The type, out_elt_size, plan and previous_dimension locals are gone. previous_dimension became group_end, the end of the range of arrays that share dimensions.
    • I moved rray_split_elt_names() below the kernels so the file reads in call order.

  • bench/split.R: new benchmark with 12 cases, including 100,000 tiny arrays with and without names.

Benchmarks (median ms, 3 alternating rounds against a main worktree)

Case main branch
Many small columns [10, 1e5] 4.59 4.53
Many small rows [1e5, 10] 4.78 4.84
Many small columns, row names 10.8 10.6
Many small columns, column names 12.1 11.8

All other cases were within noise. I reran the one noisy case ("rows with short runs") and list storage with 100 iterations, and both matched main.

How I got there

  • Building the iterator once per output array: my first version did this, the way combine builds one per input. It was 14% to 22% slower on the tiny-array cases, so I asked you and you chose build once and reuse.

  • Setting dim in a second pass: with one iterator per range of arrays, the tiny-array cases were still 5% to 10% slower. Revisiting 100,000 freshly allocated arrays to set dim missed the cache, so I moved the dim call into the kernel.

dimnames are still set in the loop in rray_split(), after the kernel runs. The named benchmarks show that costs nothing measurable.

I saved a memory note about both issues, since any other function that creates many small arrays will hit them too.

Yes, moving dim made the difference. Before, I was comparing two different scripts run at different times. This time I built the two versions side by side, differing only in where dim is set (I checked the diff), and alternated them with main: 4 rounds of 3 runs, 60 iterations each.

Median ms main dim outside the kernel dim inside (committed)
Many small columns 4.45 to 4.62 4.82 to 5.16 4.43 to 4.55
Many small rows 4.67 to 4.79 5.23 to 5.75 4.71 to 4.82

The ranges don't overlap in any of the 12 runs. Setting dim in the outer loop costs about 8% on columns and 13% on rows.

You're right that the names code still revisits out_elt there. That's why dim costs extra in the outer loop:

  • Unnamed arrays: the outer loop now only calls r_list_get(out, i). That reads the pointer from the list without touching the array itself. Setting dim there writes into each array's header, long after it was allocated.

  • Named arrays: the second pass does touch every array to set dimnames, so that path still pays the revisit. It didn't show in the benchmark (10.6 vs. 10.8 ms and 11.8 vs. 12.1 ms against main), probably because building and setting the names costs about 6 ms on its own and hides it.

The cache-miss explanation is still my best guess, not something I measured directly. The timing difference is solid. The other likely cause would be allocation order: with dim outside, the attribute cells are allocated after all the arrays instead of next to each one.

If you want the named path to avoid the revisit too, the shared case (names only on the other axes, the same dimnames object for every array) could be set inside the kernel by passing it in. Names on the split axis need a new names list per array, so that one would stay in rray_split(). I haven't done this, since the named benchmarks already match main.

I removed the temporary worktrees. Nothing in the branch changed.

Davis

Why not pre compute the output elt size and allocate out elt before the fill function like before? Then you never pull from out

Claude

That's essentially my first version, which was 14% to 22% slower on the many-small-arrays cases.

If rray_split() allocates each out_elt and then calls the fill function on it, the fill function runs once per output array. The iterator has to be built inside that function, so it gets built once per array too. That rebuild was the original slowdown: coalescing plus small memcpy() / bzero() calls, about 6 to 10 ns per array. Precomputing the size doesn't change that. My first version did the allocation inside the kernel, but the work per array was the same as what you're describing.

Median ms main One iterator per array (first version) One iterator per group (committed)
Many small columns 4.58 5.2 4.5
Many small rows 4.78 5.85 4.77

Those first-version numbers come from my first 3-round benchmark run, not today's side-by-side run.

To build the iterator only once, one call has to cover all the arrays in a group. So the arrays have to be allocated either inside that call, which is what's committed, or all before it. Allocating them all before it brings the second pass back, just reversed: allocate and set attributes for the whole group, then fill. Each array would get its data written long after it was allocated, the same pattern that made the outer-loop dim 8% to 13% slower.

There's a third option: build the iterator once in rray_split() and pass a pointer to it into a per-array fill function. That avoids both costs, but it's exactly what the header comment and the plan warn against. If the compiler doesn't inline the fill function, the loop has to reload the iterator's fields through the pointer on every element. The plan measured that as up to 5x slower for raw.

So the committed shape is the one that keeps one iterator per group, writes each array while it's still in cache, and keeps the iterator local to the kernel. Reading from out with r_list_get() afterwards is cheap because it doesn't touch the array.

If you'd like a direct number for your exact variant rather than my first-version proxy, I can build it in a worktree and benchmark it against the committed version.

Davis

The main issue is that you can’t create the iterator out front and then pass it into fill right? Because we are afraid of performance issues?

Claude

Yes. Building the iterator up front and passing it into a per-array fill() would be the simplest shape: allocate out_elt and set its attributes in rray_split(), then fill it, with no second pass and no rebuild. The only reason not to is speed, and the plan measured it:

  • Through the pointer: if fill() reads it->end and it->v_strides[0] through the pointer inside its loop, C lets a write through Rbyte* (raw) change any memory. That includes the iterator, so the compiler reloads its fields on every element and can't vectorize. The plan measured raw broadcasting up to 5.2x slower, and a strided mean 11% slower. Double output is unaffected, so the problem doesn't show up in the usual tests.

  • Copied into a local: copying the struct into a local inside fill() avoids the aliasing, but the plan measured that 7% to 30% slower on short walks. The struct is about 34 KB, mostly the stride array sized for 64 inputs.

  • Fields read into locals at each run: this was fast, the fastest in some cases. But it's a rule every loop has to follow, and breaking it only hurts raw.

There's one nuance for split. Every kernel already gets inlined into rray_split(), as the disassembly showed. Once inlined, the iterator's address never escapes, so the aliasing problem probably wouldn't happen. But that depends on the compiler choosing to inline. If it doesn't (another compiler, or a bigger function), raw would quietly get much slower. The header asks for the iterator to be built inside the function that uses it for exactly this reason.

If you'd prefer the simpler shape, I can build the pointer version and benchmark it, especially raw, against the committed version. If it matches, the remaining question is whether you're comfortable depending on inlining.

Davis

I prefer the simpler shape (way more similar to original split!!) and would be willing to accept raw slowdown if other types are just as fast

Claude

Switching to the simpler shape: build the iterator in rray_split() when the dimension changes, allocate and set attributes there like the original, and pass &it to rray_split_fill(). I'll add a raw case to the benchmark so the raw cost is measured.

Write src/split.c 
#include "split.h"

#include "axes.h"
#include "dimensionality.h"
#include "dimensions.h"
#include "strided-iterator2.h"
#include "strides.h"
#include "utils.h"

#include "decl/split-decl.h"

r_obj* ffi_rray_split(
  r_obj* ffi_x,
  r_obj* ffi_axis,
  r_obj* ffi_dimensions,
  r_obj* ffi_frame
) {
  struct r_lazy error_call = {.x = ffi_frame, .env = r_null};
  const int axis = arg_as_int(ffi_axis, rray_args.axis, error_call);
  return rray_split(ffi_x, axis, ffi_dimensions, rray_args.x, error_call);
}

r_obj* rray_split(
  r_obj* x,
  int axis,
  r_obj* dimensions,
  struct rray_arg* arg,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {
  check_unclassed(x, arg, error_call);
  x = KEEP(arg_as_array(x, arg, error_call));

  r_obj* x_dimensions = KEEP(rray_dimensions(x, arg, error_call));
  const int* v_x_dimensions = r_int_cbegin(x_dimensions);
  const int dimensionality = rray_dimensionality_from_dimensions(x_dimensions);
  check_dimensionality(dimensionality);

  check_axis(axis, dimensionality, rray_args.axis, error_call);
  const int axis_dimension = v_x_dimensions[axis - 1];

  dimensions = KEEP(arg_as_non_negative_bare_integer(
    dimensions,
    rray_args.dimensions,
    error_call
  ));
  check_split_dimensions(
    dimensions,
    axis_dimension,
    rray_args.dimensions,
    error_call
  );
  const int* v_dimensions = r_int_cbegin(dimensions);

  // i.e. each array is the same dimension and splits `axis` evenly
  const bool uniform = r_length(dimensions) == 1;

  const r_ssize out_size =
    uniform ? axis_dimension / v_dimensions[0] : r_length(dimensions);

  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_alloc_list(out_size));

  r_obj* x_names = r_dim_names(x);
  r_obj* const* v_x_names = (x_names == r_null) ? NULL : r_list_cbegin(x_names);

  r_obj* x_axis_names = (v_x_names == NULL) ? r_null : v_x_names[axis - 1];
  r_obj* const* v_x_axis_names =
    (x_axis_names == r_null) ? NULL : r_chr_cbegin(x_axis_names);

  r_ssize v_x_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  rray_fill_strides_from_dimensions(
    v_x_dimensions,
    dimensionality,
    v_x_strides
  );
  const r_ssize x_axis_stride = v_x_strides[axis - 1];

  const enum r_type type = r_typeof(x);

  // Initialized on the first iteration. Changes any time the output dimension
  // along `axis` changes, but in the uniform case all arrays share the same
  // dimensions object!
  r_obj* out_elt_dimensions = r_null;
  r_keep_loc out_elt_dimensions_loc;
  KEEP_HERE(out_elt_dimensions, &out_elt_dimensions_loc);

  struct rray_run_iterator it;

  int previous_dimension = -1;

  // The location along the axis. For example, with a 2x10 array split along
  // columns this runs from 0-9. The `x_axis_stride` maps it to `x_start`, a
  // flat location into `x` itself.
  int x_axis_start = 0;

  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < out_size; ++i) {
    const int dimension = uniform ? v_dimensions[0] : v_dimensions[i];

    if (dimension != previous_dimension) {
      previous_dimension = dimension;

      out_elt_dimensions = r_alloc_integer(dimensionality);
      KEEP_AT(out_elt_dimensions, out_elt_dimensions_loc);
      int* v_out_elt_dimensions = r_int_begin(out_elt_dimensions);
      r_memcpy(
        v_out_elt_dimensions,
        v_x_dimensions,
        sizeof(int) * dimensionality
      );
      v_out_elt_dimensions[axis - 1] = dimension;

      it = rray_run_iterator1(v_out_elt_dimensions, dimensionality, v_x_strides);
    }

    r_obj* out_elt = r_alloc_vector(type, rray_run_iterator_size(&it));
    r_list_poke(out, i, out_elt);
    r_attrib_poke_dim(out_elt, out_elt_dimensions);

    const r_ssize x_start = x_axis_start * x_axis_stride;
    rray_split_fill(x, out_elt, x_start, &it);

    if (v_x_axis_names != NULL) {
      r_obj* out_elt_names = KEEP(rray_split_elt_names(
        v_x_names,
        dimensionality,
        axis,
        v_x_axis_names,
        x_axis_start,
        dimension
      ));
      r_attrib_poke_dim_names(out_elt, out_elt_names);
      FREE(1);
    } else if (x_names != r_null) {
      r_attrib_poke_dim_names(out_elt, x_names);
    }

    x_axis_start += dimension;
  }

  FREE(5);
  return out;
}

static void check_split_dimensions(
  r_obj* dimensions,
  int axis_dimension,
  struct rray_arg* arg,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {
  const r_ssize size = r_length(dimensions);
  const int* v_dimensions = r_int_cbegin(dimensions);

  if (size == 1) {
    const int dimension = v_dimensions[0];

    if (dimension == 0) {
      r_abort_lazy_call(
        error_call,
        "A single %s value must be positive, not 0.",
        rray_arg_format(arg)
      );
    }

    if (axis_dimension % dimension != 0) {
      r_abort_lazy_call(
        error_call,
        "A single %s value of %d must evenly divide the `axis` dimension "
        "of %d.",
        rray_arg_format(arg),
        dimension,
        axis_dimension
      );
    }
  } else {
    r_ssize total_dimension = 0;

    for (r_ssize i = 0; i < size; ++i) {
      total_dimension += v_dimensions[i];
    }

    if (total_dimension != axis_dimension) {
      r_abort_lazy_call(
        error_call,
        "%s must sum to the `axis` dimension of %d, not %" R_PRI_SSIZE ".",
        rray_arg_format(arg),
        axis_dimension,
        total_dimension
      );
    }
  }
}

static void rray_split_fill(
  r_obj* x,
  r_obj* out,
  r_ssize x_start,
  struct rray_run_iterator* it
) {
  switch (r_typeof(x)) {
  case R_TYPE_logical:
    rray_split_fill_lgl(x, out, x_start, it);

Benchmarks show the simple shape matches main except when dimensions change every iteration (35% slower, due to a 34 KB struct copy from the temporary iterator), while raw stays within acceptable overhead. I'll try an initializer that writes directly into the existing iterator to eliminate that copy before showing you.

Bash: sed -n 166,262p src/strided-iterator2.h 
sed -n 166,262p src/strided-iterator2.h
static inline struct rray_run_iterator rray_run_iterator(
  const int* v_dimensions,
  int dimensionality,
  const r_ssize* const* v_v_strides,
  r_ssize n
) {
  check_dimensionality(dimensionality);

  if (n < 1 || n > RRAY_MAX_INPUTS) {
    r_stop_internal(
      "`n` (%" R_PRI_SSIZE ") must be between 1 and %d.",
      n,
      RRAY_MAX_INPUTS
    );
  }

  struct rray_run_iterator it;

  it.size = rray_size_from_dimensions_checked(
    v_dimensions,
    dimensionality,
    r_lazy_null
  );

  for (int axis = 0; axis < dimensionality; ++axis) {
    it.v_dimensions[axis] = (r_ssize) v_dimensions[axis];
  }

  // Reorder strides to be contiguous during next() calls
  for (int axis = 0; axis < dimensionality; ++axis) {
    r_ssize* v_strides = it.v_strides + axis * n;
    for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
      v_strides[i] = v_v_strides[i][axis];
    }
  }

  it.dimensionality = rray__run_iterator_axes_coalesce(
    it.v_dimensions,
    it.v_strides,
    n,
    dimensionality
  );

  it.start = 0;
  it.end = it.v_dimensions[0];

  it.n = n;

  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
    it.v_loc[i] = 0;
  }

  for (int axis = 0; axis < it.dimensionality; ++axis) {
    it.v_point[axis] = 0;
  }

  return it;
}

static inline struct rray_run_iterator rray_run_iterator1(
  const int* v_dimensions,
  int dimensionality,
  const r_ssize* v_strides
) {
  const r_ssize* v_v_strides[] = {v_strides};
  return rray_run_iterator(v_dimensions, dimensionality, v_v_strides, 1);
}

static inline struct rray_run_iterator rray_run_iterator2(
  const int* v_dimensions,
  int dimensionality,
  const r_ssize* v_strides1,
  const r_ssize* v_strides2
) {
  const r_ssize* v_v_strides[] = {v_strides1, v_strides2};
  return rray_run_iterator(v_dimensions, dimensionality, v_v_strides, 2);
}

static inline void rray_run_iterator_reset(struct rray_run_iterator* it) {
  it->start = 0;
  it->end = it->v_dimensions[0];

  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < it->n; ++i) {
    it->v_loc[i] = 0;
  }

  for (int axis = 0; axis < it->dimensionality; ++axis) {
    it->v_point[axis] = 0;
  }
}

static inline r_ssize rray_run_iterator_size(
  const struct rray_run_iterator* it
) {
  return it->size;
}

Prototyping an in-place rray_run_iterator_init() in the header, with rray_run_iterator() as a thin wrapper around it.

Bash: cat > /tmp/split-focus2.R <<'EOF'

devtools::load_all(quiet = TRUE)
xl <- array(as.list(seq_len(100000L)), c(1000L, 100L))
xr <- array(as.raw(seq_len(1e6L) %% 256L), c(1000L, 1000L))
xe <- array(runif(1e6L), c(1000L, 1000L)); de <- rep(c(1L, 3L), times = 250L)
for (r in 1:3) {
  b <- bench::mark(
    list = rray_split(xl, axis = 1L, dimensions = 100L),
    raw = rray_split(xr, axis = 1L, dimensions = 100L),
    explicit = rray_split(xe, axis = 2L, dimensions = de),
    iterations = 100L, memory = FALSE, check = FALSE
  )
  print(b[c("expression", "median")])
}

I've built the simple shape, but it isn't as fast as main for every non-raw type: list is about 10% slower. It's uncommitted in the working tree. The committed group version is still on the branch and matches main everywhere.

What the simple shape looks like

  • rray_split() is back to main's structure. It keeps previous_dimension and type, allocates out_elt and sets dim and dimnames in the loop, then calls rray_split_fill(x, out_elt, x_start, &it).

  • The kernels take struct rray_run_iterator* it, reset it, and walk it. They're as short as main's.

  • It needed one header change. it = rray_run_iterator1(...) copied the whole 34 KB struct every time the dimension changed, which made explicit dimensions 35% slower. I split the constructor into an in-place rray_run_iterator_init(&it, ...), and rray_run_iterator() is now a thin wrapper around it. The machine code for the other kernels is unchanged apart from one equivalent compare instruction.

Benchmarks against main (all 14 cases, 3 rounds, then 6 more rounds of 100 iterations on the close ones)

  • Every double and integer case, the named cases, character and explicit dimensions match main.

  • Raw: about 5% slower (117 to 123 µs vs. 111 to 118 µs).

  • List: about 10% slower (462 to 476 µs vs. 415 to 433 µs), consistent in all 6 rounds.

Why list is slower

main keeps the per-run walking state in registers. In the simple shape, the iterator's changing fields (start, end, v_loc, v_point) live in the big struct on rray_split()'s stack, so every run reloads and stores them. The list inner loop itself is identical. I can't fully explain why that costs list and raw but not double, which has 10 times as many runs in "rows with short runs".

Your options

  1. Take the simple shape and accept list about 10% and raw about 5% slower.

  2. Keep the committed group version. It matches main on every type, but its structure is less like the original.

  3. Let me dig further into getting the run state back into registers in the simple shape. That probably means reading v_loc and v_point into locals in the fill function, which changes how the iterator is used, so I'd only try it with your go-ahead.

I've left the main worktree at /tmp/rray4-main for further benchmarking.

Write ~/.claude/projects/-Users-davis-files-r-packages-rray4/memory/project_many_small_o… 
---
name: project-many-small-outputs
description: For functions producing many small arrays (split, unstack), the user prefers a shared iterator passed by pointer over a faster but restructured kernel
metadata:
  type: project
---

`rray_split()` builds one `rray_run_iterator` per distinct dimension in the driver, using `rray_run_iterator_init(&it, ...)`, and passes `&it` to per-element fill functions that call `rray_run_iterator_reset()` first. This keeps the original split structure (allocate and set attributes in the driver, then fill).

Measured costs that led here (Oct 2026, branch `feature/split-iterator`):

- Building an iterator per output array costs about 6 to 10 ns each, which is 14% to 22% on 1e5 tiny arrays.

- `it = rray_run_iterator1(...)` copies the whole 34 KB struct on each assignment. Use `rray_run_iterator_init()` to build in place.

- Allocating all arrays first and setting `dim` in a second pass misses the cache, about 8% to 13% slower.

- The shared iterator shape is about 10% slower for list and 5% slower for raw, because the run state stays on the stack instead of in registers. The user accepted this for the simpler shape.

**Why:** the user prefers code that stays close to the original structure, and accepts small slowdowns on less common types for it.

**How to apply:** for similar conversions, start from the shared iterator plus `init()` and `reset()` shape, and benchmark against main, see [[project-benchmarking-against-main]].

No, I wouldn't. The struct copy only happens when you reassign an iterator that already exists, and split is the only place that does that.

I checked without changing anything:

  • Every other call site creates the iterator once, as struct rray_run_iterator it = rray_run_iterator2(...). That covers broadcast.c, combine.c, index.c and permute-axes.c, plus the binary.h and reduce.h macros. When you declare and build in the same line, the compiler writes the fields straight into it, so there's nothing to copy.

  • No object file in src/ contains the 34 KB memcpy() anymore. Before the change it only appeared in split.o, at the it = ... reassignment.

  • When I added init(), the other kernels' machine code stayed the same, apart from one equivalent compare instruction. So switching them to init() would gain nothing.

Using the value constructor elsewhere also has real benefits:

  • It's one line, and the iterator can't be used before it's built.

  • rray_run_iterator1() and rray_run_iterator2() keep the call sites short. With only init(), every caller would need its own v_v_strides array and an explicit n, or we'd add init1() and init2() and have two sets of wrappers.

The rule I'd write down: build with rray_run_iterator() (or the 1 and 2 wrappers), and use rray_run_iterator_init() only to rebuild an iterator that already exists. That fits split today. It may also fit the nested reducers when they move over, if an inner iterator's dimensions change between outputs. If only its starting location changes, reset() is enough.

If you want the rule enforced, the next step would be a short header comment on init() saying it's for rebuilding. Under the project rules you'd write that comment, not me.

Davis

Hmm but can’t you just remove the foot gun altogether if init (and 1/2) is the ONLY way to create one?

Claude

Yes. If init() is the only way to build one, you can't write the slow reassignment by accident. I think that's the better design, and it costs less than I suggested.

What it fixes

  • With a value constructor, struct rray_run_iterator it = rray_run_iterator2(...) is fast, but the nearly identical it = rray_run_iterator2(...) copies 34 KB. Nothing warns you, and I only found it through a benchmark.

  • With init() only, that line can't be written. The remaining way to copy is it = other_it, which nobody would write by accident.

  • It's also more honest about the iterator. It's a 34 KB object that should never be passed or returned by value, and taking a pointer makes that clear.

What it costs

  • Each call site becomes two lines instead of one:

    struct rray_run_iterator it;
rray_run_iterator_init2(&it, v_dimensions, dimensionality, v_x_strides, v_y_strides);

  • The iterator exists briefly before it's built. In practice init() comes right after the declaration, and clang's -Wuninitialized catches use before initialization in simple cases.

  • rray_run_iterator1() and rray_run_iterator2() would become rray_run_iterator_init1() and rray_run_iterator_init2(), so the short call sites stay short.

Speed

When I made the constructor a wrapper around init(), the other kernels' machine code stayed the same apart from one equivalent compare. So I'd expect calling init() directly to compile the same. I'd confirm that by comparing the disassembly of every touched object file before and after, rather than assume it.

Scope

  • Call sites: the binary.h and reduce.h macros, broadcast.c, combine.c, index.c, permute-axes.c and split.c.

  • The header loses rray_run_iterator() and its two wrappers, and gains init1() and init2().

This is a mechanical change, so I'd do it as its own commit or PR rather than mix it into the split work.

I haven't changed anything. Do you want it on this branch, or on a new branch stacked on top of it?

Davis

Write plans/init.md 
# Build run iterators only with `init()`

Today there are two ways to build a `rray_run_iterator`:

- Return it by value: `rray_run_iterator()`, `rray_run_iterator1()` and `rray_run_iterator2()`.

- Fill an existing one through a pointer: `rray_run_iterator_init()`, added for `rray_split()`.

This plan removes the first way, so `init()` is the only way to build one.

---

# Why

The iterator struct is about 34 KB. Most of that is the stride array, which has room for 64 dimensions and 64 inputs.

Building it on the line where you declare it is fast. The compiler writes the fields straight into `it`:

```c
struct rray_run_iterator it = rray_run_iterator1(v_dimensions, dimensionality, v_strides);
```

Assigning it to an iterator that already exists looks almost the same, but copies the whole 34 KB every time:

```c
it = rray_run_iterator1(v_dimensions, dimensionality, v_strides);
```

Nothing warns about this. We only found it in `rray_split()` through a benchmark. There the iterator is rebuilt whenever the dimension changes, and splitting `[1000, 1000]` with `dimensions = rep(c(1, 3), 250)` was 35% slower than `main` (0.87 ms against 0.64 ms). The disassembly showed a 34,344 byte `memcpy()` on each rebuild. Switching to `init()` brought it back to `main`.

With `init()` as the only way to build an iterator, that line can't be written. The only copy left is `it = other_it`, which nobody writes by accident. Taking a pointer also says what the iterator is: a large object that is never passed or returned by value.

---

# The change

## Header

In `src/strided-iterator2.h`:

- Remove `rray_run_iterator()`, `rray_run_iterator1()` and `rray_run_iterator2()`.

- Keep `rray_run_iterator_init()`, for any number of inputs.

- Add `rray_run_iterator_init1()` and `rray_run_iterator_init2()`, which build the `v_v_strides` array and call `init()` with a literal `n`, like the wrappers they replace.

```c
static inline void rray_run_iterator_init1(
  struct rray_run_iterator* it,
  const int* v_dimensions,
  int dimensionality,
  const r_ssize* v_strides
) {
  const r_ssize* v_v_strides[] = {v_strides};
  rray_run_iterator_init(it, v_dimensions, dimensionality, v_v_strides, 1);
}
```

## Call sites

Each one goes from one statement to two:

```c
struct rray_run_iterator it;
rray_run_iterator_init2(&it, v_dimensions, dimensionality, v_x_strides, v_y_strides);
```

| File | Today | After |
|---|---|---|
| `src/binary.h` | `rray_run_iterator2()` | `rray_run_iterator_init2()` |
| `src/reduce.h` | `rray_run_iterator1()` | `rray_run_iterator_init1()` |
| `src/broadcast.c` (2) | `rray_run_iterator1()` | `rray_run_iterator_init1()` |
| `src/combine.c` (2) | `rray_run_iterator2()` | `rray_run_iterator_init2()` |
| `src/index.c` (2) | `rray_run_iterator()` | `rray_run_iterator_init()` |
| `src/permute-axes.c` (2) | `rray_run_iterator1()` | `rray_run_iterator_init1()` |
| `src/split.c` | `rray_run_iterator_init()` with a one-element `v_v_x_strides` | `rray_run_iterator_init1()` |

`src/reduce-mean.c` and `src/reduce.c` still use the old `src/strided-iterator.h` and aren't touched.

---

# Costs

- Two lines per call site instead of one.

- The iterator exists briefly before it's built. `init()` always comes right after the declaration, and clang's `-Wuninitialized` catches use before initialization in simple cases.

---

# Checking it

- Speed should not change. When `rray_run_iterator()` became a wrapper around `init()`, the code for add, broadcast, combine, compare, permute-axes and sum stayed the same, apart from one equivalent compare instruction (`cmp #16; b.hs` became `cmp #15; b.hi`).

- Confirm that rather than assume it: disassemble every object file that includes `src/strided-iterator2.h` before and after, and compare.

- Run the benchmarks in `bench/` only for files whose code changed in a way that isn't trivially equivalent.

- Run the full test suite.

---

# Scope

One mechanical commit, separate from the split work, so the review is only about the API change.

aaf491c Move rray_split() to the run iterator

I wrote plans/init.md, committed it as 5dfbdc1 ("Plan building run iterators only through init()") and pushed it to feature/split-iterator. No code changed.

The plan covers:

  • Why: reassigning an iterator silently copies 34 KB. That's what made split with alternating explicit dimensions 35% slower, and if init() is the only way to build one, that line can't be written.

  • The change: remove rray_run_iterator() and its 1 and 2 wrappers, and add rray_run_iterator_init1() and rray_run_iterator_init2(). A table lists every call site that needs updating. reduce-mean.c and reduce.c still use the old iterator, so they aren't touched.

  • Costs: two lines per call site, and a brief moment where the iterator is declared but not yet built.

  • Checking it: compare the disassembly of every object file that includes the header before and after, benchmark only where the code really changed, and run the full test suite.

  • Scope: one mechanical commit, separate from the split work.

Done. rray_split() now resets the iterator when the dimension is unchanged and rebuilds it with init1() when it changes. The fill functions only walk. This is committed as 28c6e99 ("Reset or rebuild the split iterator in rray_split() itself") and pushed.

if (dimension == previous_dimension) {
  rray_run_iterator_reset(&it);
} else {
  previous_dimension = dimension;

  rray_run_iterator_init1(&it, v_out_elt_dimensions, dimensionality, v_x_strides);

All 3497 tests pass, with no compiler warnings.

Speed, from quick runs of this build only (not alternated with main):

  • Many small columns and rows: 4.5 and 4.7 ms, the same as before.

  • List: 412 to 440 µs.

  • Raw: 106 to 115 µs.

  • Explicit dimensions: 589 to 637 µs.

The list and raw numbers are now inside main's earlier range (415 to 433 µs and 111 to 118 µs), so the slowdown you accepted may be gone. I can't claim that from runs that weren't side by side with main. If you want to know for sure, I can rerun the alternating comparison.
